The closing commit that `close` creates (post-commit or a manual retry; not a user's own commit judged by `check`) has the subject `chore(shipcraft): close lock <ids>`: the lock ids (file names without `.json`) in bytewise ascending order joined by `, `, one line ending in LF, no body or trailers (item 49; this fixes the order item 17 left free). Before item 49 the subject was `shipcraft: close lock <ids>`, outside the Conventional Commits form draken-platform's checks use. The guarantee is this literal subject, not acceptance by every project policy: a commitlint scope list without `shipcraft`, or a subject-length limit with many locks, can still refuse it, and closing commits already in history keep their old subject. No hook runs on the closing commit, as before; nothing in Shipcraft reads the subject, and a lock closed under either subject is judged by its state. A merge is judged against both parents, as in CI. A closed lock no longer restricts the tests.

### `red` and the hook keep the runner's output in a file and show its start and end (item 139)

Found on 2026-11-16 by POC-051 (simulated adopters), runs 5 (expense-compass, a Lovable React app: `red` printed about 129 KB, vitest's failure detail, the suite run twice) and 6 (barbearia, React from scratch: `red` printed 306 KB of vitest DOM dumps from failing Testing Library cases, and in the hook the closing Shipcraft line was lost in the agent's output filter), recorded in the POC-051 card (`poc/051-simulated-adopters/FICHA.md`, item 6) and in item 131's list of later items. Agents read this output: the cost is their context and missed verdicts, not looks. Victor's decision (2026-11-18, "Pode resolver, tá?").

**Reproduction, on main (2.78.1),** in a scratch copy of the barbearia scaffold (React 19, vitest 5, jsdom, Testing Library, `src/test-setup.ts`), `init` committed, then a RED of 10 cases (8 `getByRole` queries for buttons the page does not have, each failure dumping the rendered DOM, one plain assertion on a stub, one passing case):
- `shipcraft red`: exit 0, 249,205 bytes in all: 248,551 on stderr (5,614 lines: `[build]`'s `tsc -b`, then vitest twice, each about 124 KB) and 654 on stdout (Shipcraft's 7 verdict lines, which come last). Of the 11,228 stderr lines, 3 are Shipcraft's.
- The RED commit through the hook: 124,534 bytes, 5,618 lines; Shipcraft's `RED commit checked: ...` is line 5,613.
- Nothing in the output is needed for the verdict: `red` and the hook read the JUnit report (item 13), and a refusal's reason quotes only the first non-blank line of a stream (items 16a.2, 45).

A prototype of point 1 below on the same RED printed 12,610 bytes (`red`) with vitest's closing summary (`Test Files ... Tests 9 failed | 1 passed`) on screen, and wrote all 249,141 runner bytes to the file.

The first RED case reproduces it on main without vitest: a suite command that writes 300,000 bytes to stdout and stderr and a JUnit report with one failing case; `red` prints more than 20,000 bytes in all (stdout plus stderr) on 2.78.1, and must print at most 20,000 after this item.

Everything in items 13, 16a, 22, 45, 53, 55, 57, 60, 98, 131 and 134 holds unless a point below changes it: which commands run, in which tree, with which environment, the JUnit report as the only verdict, the refusal codes and their reasons, the rows, and the order and text of Shipcraft's own lines.

1. **`red` and the hook show each command's first 4,096 bytes and its last lines, and keep every byte in a file.**
   - **Where.** Every command Shipcraft starts and today forwards to its stderr (`suite::shell`: `[build]`, each suite's `check`, each suite's test command, on every run: the narrowed first run, the full run, the second run, the redo's runs, the hook's run) during `shipcraft red` (with or without `--redo` and its other options) and `shipcraft check` (with or without `--commit-msg`: the hook). Not during `ci`, whose output stays today's (GitHub keeps CI logs, and the git dir of a CI runner is gone with it; a later item), nor `init`'s quiet `[build]` probe (item 131), which stays quiet.
   - **The file.** `F` is `git rev-parse --git-path shipcraft/output/red.log` for `red`, `shipcraft/output/hook.log` for the hook, run at the repository root and read as every other git answer is read (`Repo::text`: lossy UTF-8, surrounding whitespace trimmed; a relative answer is joined to the root; an empty answer is point 3): one per worktree, inside the git directory, so never tracked. Right after the first such command of the invocation has started (its spawn succeeded), before any of its output is read, Shipcraft creates `F`'s missing parent folders and creates or truncates `F`; an invocation that starts no command (a refusal before the suites, a hook with no open lock, a spawn that fails with today's exit 2) leaves `F` as it was. For each command, Shipcraft appends one line `shipcraft: \`<command>\`` (the command exactly as passed to `sh -c`: after `{junit}`, the hook's filter and item 134's borrowed interpreter, folded to one line as the `build-failed` reason folds it), then the command's bytes in observation order (below), then a newline if those bytes did not end in one.
   - **Observation order.** Shipcraft reads stdout and stderr on two threads, as today, in chunks; each chunk, as one unit and under one lock, is counted, shown or kept for the tail, and appended to `F`. The order of chunks under that lock is the observation order: each stream's bytes keep their own order, and no order between the two streams is promised beyond that (as today's forwarding). The screen, the count `<n>`, the tail and `F` all follow this one order.
   - **The end of a command.** As today, Shipcraft waits for the command to exit, then up to two seconds for streams a leftover process holds open. What was read by then is the command's output: `<n>`, the tail and the cut line are computed from it. Bytes a leftover process writes after that deadline are appended to `F` (best effort) and are no longer shown on the screen, so they never land among later Shipcraft lines or a later command's output (today they still are forwarded).
   - **The screen.** Each command's bytes, both streams in arrival order, go to Shipcraft's stderr as they arrive, as today, until 4,096 bytes of that command have gone; the 4,096th byte may fall inside a line. The rest goes only to `F`. When the command ends (after today's two-second wait for streams a leftover process holds open), Shipcraft ends an open line with a newline, as today. Then, only when the command wrote more than 4,096 bytes, it prints to stderr:
     - the tail: the last lines of the bytes not shown, at most 40 lines and at most 4,096 bytes. Take the last 4,096 bytes of the unshown part (all of it when shorter); unless they start where the unshown part starts or right after a newline, drop everything up to and including their first newline (none: keep them all); split the rest at newlines (a last line without one counts; no empty line after a final newline) and keep the last 40. Each is printed as lossy UTF-8 followed by a newline;
     - then one line: `shipcraft: output cut: the command wrote <n> bytes; shown: the first 4096 and the last <k> line(s); all of it: <path> (SHIPCRAFT_OUTPUT=full prints everything)`, with `<n>` the command's byte count, `<k>` the tail's line count (0 when the tail is empty), and `<path>` `F` relative to the repository root when it is inside it (`.git/shipcraft/output/red.log`), else absolute (a linked worktree's `.../.git/worktrees/<name>/shipcraft/output/hook.log`).
     A command that wrote 4,096 bytes or fewer prints exactly what it prints today, with no extra line. The bound counts the command's own bytes: per command the screen gets at most 4,096 of them, then at most 4,096 for the tail (each invalid UTF-8 sequence in the tail prints as U+FFFD, three bytes, so pure binary output can print up to three times that), up to 41 newlines and the cut line. A last line longer than 4,096 bytes shows as one tail line, its last bytes (the window holds no newline, so nothing is dropped).
   - **When `F` fails mid-run.** If any write to `F` fails during a command, its cut line ends `; the file <path> is incomplete (a write failed)` instead of `; all of it: <path> (SHIPCRAFT_OUTPUT=full prints everything)`, and Shipcraft stops writing `F` for the rest of the invocation (later commands keep point 1's screen bound and say the same). The verdict and exit are unchanged.
   - **Unchanged.** The first-line readers (`FirstLine` for `no-report`'s and the check's reasons, item 45's stdout fallback) read the whole of both streams as today, shown or not. The JUnit report, the lock, its cases, the controls and every verdict are the same bytes as with today's forwarding. Shipcraft's own lines (`running the suite`, `checking the project`, `running the build` before each command; `red`'s and the hook's verdict lines on stdout after the runs; refusals after an ended line, section 7) keep their text, stream and order; the cut line belongs to the command it follows.
2. **`SHIPCRAFT_OUTPUT=full` restores today's output.** When the environment variable `SHIPCRAFT_OUTPUT` is exactly `full` (byte for byte: no trimming, no case folding; a value that is not valid Unicode is not `full`) in the `red` or hook process, every command's output is forwarded whole as today, `F` is neither created nor touched, and no cut line prints. Any other value, or unset, is point 1. Since a git hook inherits the environment of `git commit`, `SHIPCRAFT_OUTPUT=full git commit ...` shows the hook's full output.
3. **When `F` cannot be opened, nothing is lost.** When resolving `F` fails (git cannot answer) or creating its folders or creating/truncating `F` fails, that invocation forwards every command's output whole as today, with no cut line, and goes on exactly as today: same exit, same verdict, no new refusal, no new row field.

**Feature or fix: a feature, 2.79.0.** Today's forwarding is what the spec says (section 7: "Output from a project command that `shipcraft` passes through"), and every byte was shown; this item changes what users see by design, so it is not a wrong verdict. No command, flag, config key, lock format, refusal code or row field changes; the new environment variable is optional, so it is a minor version: 2.79.0 if it merges first after 2.78.1, else the next minor after what is on main then.

**What changes for users who read the full output today.** In `red` and the hook, a command that writes more than 4 KB now shows its start and end and the path of a file that holds all of it; `SHIPCRAFT_OUTPUT=full` brings back the old output, per run. `ci` (GitHub logs and local), `pr`, `merge` and `init` are unchanged. No migration: nothing to change in any config, hook or brief.

**Known limits.** Two `red` (or two hook) invocations at once in one worktree write the same `F`, so it may mix them. `F` keeps only the last invocation. The 4,096-byte cut and the tail are per command, so `red` on a project with several suites (and `[build]`, and checks) can print up to the bound once per command. `ci`'s output in GitHub Actions and in local CI stays whole (a later item, with the same rule and a place GitHub keeps).

**Metric:** on the release, the barbearia reproduction above prints at most 20,000 bytes from `red` and from the hook, with vitest's `Tests ... failed | ... passed` summary and Shipcraft's verdict lines on screen, and `F` holds every runner byte. POC-051's second arm counts Shipcraft verdict lines an agent missed: target 0. False cut: a command of 4,096 bytes or fewer that shows a cut line: 0. Revert: an adopter who reports a failure the screen hid and `F` did not hold; then a patch makes `full` the default (point 2's behavior, no file), and `SHIPCRAFT_OUTPUT` keeps its meaning.

Estimate, 30: point 1, 22 (the reproduction first: 300,000 bytes, `red` prints at most 20,000 in all and the lock is written; the hook on the RED commit and on a GREEN commit, at most 20,000; `F` holds every byte, in order, after its `shipcraft: \`...\`` line; exactly 4,096 bytes written: today's output, no cut line; 4,097: the cut line with `<n>` 4097 and the 4,097th byte in the tail; the first 4,096 bytes on screen are the command's first 4,096; 100 short lines after the cut: the last 40, in order; lines of 300 bytes: the 4,096-byte cap and the dropped partial line; output ending without a newline; invalid UTF-8 in the tail: lossy; `red`'s two runs and `[build]` each with their own cut line and all three in `red.log` in order; `F` truncated by the next `red`; a refusal that starts no command leaves `F` as it was; the hook's `hook.log` apart from `red.log`; `no-report` with a large stderr: today's reason (first stderr line) and the cut; a failing `check` with large output: today's reason; a linked worktree: absolute path, the worktree's own `F`; the verdict lines on stdout after the cut line; the lock the same bytes as with `SHIPCRAFT_OUTPUT=full` (`red` twice, equal ids); a process left holding stdout open: the tail still prints; Node and Go runners: the same cut); point 2, 4 (`full` on `red` and on the hook: every byte on screen, `F` untouched; `FULL` or `1`: point 1); point 3, 2 (a file where `shipcraft/output` should be a folder: today's output, exit and lock unchanged; a read-only `F`: the same); `ci` with 300,000 bytes: all of them, no cut line, no `F` (control); `init`'s probe: unchanged (control, item 131's quiet case). The RED may trade cases between points within 40.

**Current behavior** on these paths (keep unless marked change):
- `suite::shell` runs a command with `sh -c` in its root, both streams piped and forwarded to Shipcraft's stderr chunk by chunk as they arrive, keeps the first non-blank line of each stream (`FirstLine`, at most 4,096 bytes), waits up to two seconds for streams a leftover process holds open, and ends an open line with a newline. Change: in `red` and the hook, point 1's bound and file. `shell_with(..., pass = false)` (`init`'s probe): keep.
- `suite::build`, `run`, `run_commands`, `run_changed`, `run_red` print `shipcraft: running the build (...)`, `checking the project (...)`, `running the suite (...)` on stderr before each command; keep.
- `red` prints its verdict lines on stdout after its runs (controls, `red also locked ...`, `RED locked at ...` / `RED redone at ...`, `commit the lock ...`, `next: the GREEN ...`), and the hook its lines on stdout (`no open lock; commit allowed.`, `RED commit checked: ...`, `locked cases still fail; ...`, `lock ... closes in this commit.`, `all locked cases pass; ...`); keep.
- `ci` runs `[build]`, checks and suites through the same `shell`, forwarding everything; keep.
- Rows: `medicao::record` appends to `runs.jsonl` under the shared git directory's `shipcraft/` best effort; `red` and the hook write their rows as today; keep (no new field).
- `red` and the hook create `.git/shipcraft/tmp` for the selection lists (item 22) and remove the lists after each run; JUnit reports go to `TMPDIR` and are removed after reading; keep. New: `shipcraft/output/` under the per-worktree git path, kept between runs.
- The hook (`check::run`): with `--commit-msg` it first removes the per-worktree `shipcraft/close` mark and refuses `mark-failed` when it cannot, and writes the mark again when the commit closes a lock; plain `check` writes and stages the closed lock; `warn_outside` prints its warnings about lock files outside the commit before any suite runs; the post-commit close keeps a mark for retry when it cannot close and reports index or tree failures as today. All kept; the hook may judge an export of the index under `TMPDIR` while `F` stays the original worktree's.
- Running: checks, suites and `[build]` run with the caller's `TMPDIR`; `red` and an eligible hook run in the working tree, and `[build]` leaves its output there; `red::with_lists` removes a selection folder it created empty, the hook does not; the export folder is removed best effort. All kept.
- Standing effects the cases can meet, all kept: `red` and `red --redo` write `.shipcraft/locks/<id>.json`; the hook writes a row on every commit and may run on an export of the index under `TMPDIR`; the post-commit hook makes `chore(shipcraft): close lock <id>`; `init` writes its files.

### GREEN may add tests

New tests written during GREEN are not in the open lock. They become contract at the next RED, and CI runs them with the full suite.

### Support files are locked

The globs `init` writes include each language's support files, so GREEN cannot empty a test through a helper: `conftest.py` and test `fixtures/`/`helpers/` (Python), runner config and `__tests__/` (Node), `testdata/` (Go). They are plain globs in the same list as the tests.

### The files vitest reads beside the tests are locked (item 132)

Wrong-verdict note. In v2.0.0 (`nucleo/src/lang.rs`'s Node profile, `git tag --contains 4f9d784`, the oldest tag): the Node globs lock `**/vitest.config.*` and `**/jest.config.*` but not the files a vitest config names, nor a `vite.config.*` vitest reads when no `vitest.config.*` exists. Found on 2026-11-18 by POC-051 run 6 (`runs/6-barbearia/log.md`: the lock held `.shipcraft.toml`, the tests and `vitest.config.ts`, not `src/test-setup.ts`), recorded by item 131's spec (commit `d36889f`, 2026-11-18T21:10-03:00). The layer that should have caught it is section 1's guarantee ("its support files, or the config that says how the suite runs"): 2.0.0's globs listed Python's `conftest.py` for that reason, and no case pinned the Node equivalent.

**Reproduction, on main (2.74.0, the shared binary):** two scratch repositories with vitest installed and `init`'s Node config (`node_modules/.bin/vitest`, 2.0.0's globs), each with one failing case `src/sum.test.ts::adds` against a stub `src/sum.ts` that throws.
- (A) `vitest.config.ts` with `test: { setupFiles: ['./src/test-setup.ts'] }`. `red` locks 3 files (`.shipcraft.toml`, `src/sum.test.ts`, `vitest.config.ts`). A GREEN commit that changes only `src/test-setup.ts`, to `vi.mock` the module under test with a `sum` that returns 3, passes the hook: `all locked cases pass`, and the lock closes. `src/sum.ts` still throws.
- (B) no `vitest.config.*`; `vite.config.ts` with `test: { include: [...] }`. `red` locks 2 files (`.shipcraft.toml`, `src/sum.test.ts`). A GREEN commit that adds `setupFiles: ['./src/setup.ts']` to `vite.config.ts` and a new `src/setup.ts` with the same mock passes the hook and closes the lock. (Excluding the case in `vite.config.ts` instead is caught today: `caso-sumiu`.)

The first RED case reproduces (A) on main: it fails on 2.74.0 because the hook accepts the commit.

Everything in items 13, 43, 46, 53, 55, 60, 65 and 98 holds unless a point below changes it: what the hook and CI judge (every path in the lock's `files`, by hash), the existing refusal codes, the lock format, the selection, the controls and the second run. `red` and `red --redo` hash more paths into `files`, `red` gains one refusal and one line, and the hook and CI gain one refusal.

1. **The runner configs, the modules they import and the setup files they name are locked.**
   - **Which suites.** A *vitest suite* is a suite whose `command` contains `vitest` (the test `suite.rs` already uses to pick its runner). For every other suite nothing changes. Suites are taken together: a path found through two suites is found once.
   - **Runner configs.** A *runner config name* is a last path component `vitest.config.<ext>`, `vitest.workspace.<ext>` or `vite.config.<ext>`, with `<ext>` one of `ts`, `mts`, `cts`, `js`, `mjs`, `cjs`. A *candidate* is a path of `repo.files()` (the list `test_files` reads: tracked, plus untracked files git does not ignore) with no path component `node_modules` and a runner config name. The *locked runner configs* of a vitest suite are: every candidate under the suite's root (every path when the root is the project's) named `vitest.config.<ext>` or `vitest.workspace.<ext>`; and a candidate named `vite.config.<ext>` directly in the suite's root folder (not in a subfolder) when no candidate named `vitest.config.<ext>` is directly in that folder (vitest then reads its settings from `vite.config`, and a GREEN could add a `test` block, as (B) shows). A `vite.config.*` beside a `vitest.config.*`, or in a subfolder, is locked only when point 1's import rule reaches it.
   - **Config modules.** The locked runner configs are *config modules*, and so is every path reached from a config module through a *relative import*: the literal (below) of `from <literal>`, `import <literal>`, `import(<literal>)` or `require(<literal>)`, outside comments, whose content starts with `./` or `../`, resolved as a setup literal is; the keyword (`from`, `import`, `require`) must itself be outside comments and strings. A reached path whose extension is not one of `ts`, `tsx`, `mts`, `cts`, `js`, `jsx`, `mjs`, `cjs` (a `.json`) is locked but not scanned. This follows `mergeConfig(viteConfig, ...)`, a shared `./config/base.ts`, and options imported from another file, to any depth. A bare import (`vitest/config`, `@vitejs/plugin-react`) is not followed.
   - **The scan.** Each config module is read with `fs::read` and scanned as JavaScript text (lossy UTF-8): `//` to the end of the line and `/* ... */` are comments; `'...'`, `"..."` and `` `...` `` are strings (a backslash escapes the next character). A *literal* is a string with no backslash, no CR or LF and, in backticks, no `${`. A *setup key* is, outside comments and strings, the word `setupFiles` or `globalSetup` (the byte before, if any, and the byte after are none of `A`-`Z`, `a`-`z`, `0`-`9`, `_`, `$`), or a string whose whole content is one of those words. The word `root` (same byte test, or a string whose whole content is `root`) is a *root key* only when, after comments and whitespace, `:` follows it (a property); `const root = ...` and `path.resolve('root')` are not root keys. A regular expression literal is not recognized: it is scanned as text (a known limit). A setup key is *read* when, after it and comments and whitespace, comes `:`, then a *value* (one literal, or `[`, literals separated by `,`, an optional trailing `,`, `]`, with comments and whitespace allowed between tokens), then, after comments and whitespace, `,` or `}`.
   - **Setup files.** Each literal of a read setup key names a path: its content joined to the config module's folder, with `.` and empty segments dropped and each `..` removing the segment before it. A literal above the repository root, or with no repository file among its candidates, names nothing (a package such as `@testing-library/jest-dom/vitest`). The candidates are the path itself, then the path followed by `.ts`, `.tsx`, `.mts`, `.cts`, `.js`, `.jsx`, `.mjs`, `.cjs`; every candidate that is a path of `repo.files()` and not a folder is locked (all of them, not only the first; a folder candidate is skipped). A literal whose content starts with `./` or `../` and that has no such candidate (a missing file, or one git ignores, such as a generated setup) is unreadable (point 2), as is a relative import with none.
   - **What qualifies.** A candidate for a runner config, a config module or a setup file that `fs::symlink_metadata` says is a symbolic link makes its config module unreadable (point 2). A read error on a config module, or `repo.files()` failing, makes `red` fail as `locked_files` fails today (exit 2, no lock written). The test files keep today's reading (`config.test_files`, item 30's links and folders, `suite-without-tests`): these rules apply only to the paths this point adds.
   - **The discovery** runs to the end (every reachable module scanned) before point 2 judges, so its result does not depend on traversal order.
   - **The lock.** `files` holds, as today, every test file and `.shipcraft.toml`, plus the config modules and the setup files, each once, hashed with `fs::read` as today. The `RED locked at` and `RED redone at` counts include them. `red --redo` computes the set again from the tree, as it does the test files.
2. **When Shipcraft cannot read a config module, `red` refuses.**
   - A config module is *unreadable* when it holds a setup key that is not read (a variable, a function call, a spread, a concatenation such as `'./a' + 'b.ts'` or `[...].concat(x)`, `setupFiles.push(...)`, an assignment, `const setupFiles = [...]`, which a shorthand `{ setupFiles }` could pass on), a literal of a read setup key whose content starts with `/`, a relative literal or import with no candidate (point 1), a symbolic link candidate, an `import(` or `require(` outside comments and strings whose argument is not one literal followed by `)` (a variable or a built path, which Shipcraft cannot follow), or a root key whose value is anything but `import.meta.dirname`, `__dirname`, `'.'` or `'./'` followed by `,` or `}` (those name the config's own folder, the folder setup paths are resolved from: items 51 and 71's package configs write `root:import.meta.dirname`).
   - `red` and `red --redo` then refuse with code `setup-unreadable` where `locked_files` runs today (after the first run, so the run's outputs, temporaries and row are as today), before the lock is written or replaced, naming the first unreadable config module in bytewise path order:
     `shipcraft refused: could not read the vitest setup files of <config>: <why>`
     `Why: red locks the setup files it can name; one it cannot name could change what every locked case sees`
     `Next: in <config>, write setupFiles and globalSetup as string literals or arrays of them, with paths relative to the file and no root key, then run red again`
     `<why>` is, for the first of these the file holds in text order: `<key> is not a string or an array of strings`, `<literal> is an absolute path`, `<literal> names no file in the repository`, `<path> is a symbolic link`, `an import is not a string literal`, or `it sets root`. `<config>`, `<literal>` and `<path>` go through `red::one_line`.
   - This is the stricter path the threat model asks for: locking a broader set by name would still miss a setup file with another name and lock product files named like one, and a notice would let the escape through.
3. **`red` names what it locked beyond the globs.** When the lock's `files` holds paths that match no suite's globs and are not `.shipcraft.toml`, `red` and `red --redo` print one stdout line: `shipcraft: red also locked what vitest reads beside the tests: <list>`, where `<list>` is `short_list` of those paths, each through `red::one_line`, in bytewise order of the original paths (three names, then `and <n> more`). It is computed from the final hash pass, printed once after the lock is written, right before the `RED locked at` (or `RED redone at`) line and after item 98's controls line. A `red` that refuses or fails, and a `red --redo` that prints `RED unchanged`, print it not. With no such path the output is today's byte for byte.
4. **The hook and CI refuse a runner config added while a lock is open.** Where `check_lefthook` runs (the hook for the index, CI for each commit, for every bound lock whose change is not `Opened`), a new check refuses with code `runner-config-added` a path that, in the judged snapshot, would be a locked runner config of point 1 for a vitest suite of the snapshot's `.shipcraft.toml` (a `vitest.config.<ext>` or `vitest.workspace.<ext>` under its root, or a `vite.config.<ext>` directly in its root folder; no component `node_modules`), is absent from every parent, and is not in the lock's `files` (so `examples/demo/vite.config.ts` under a root vitest suite is not refused):
   `shipcraft refused: runner config <path> was added <place> with lock <lock> open`
   `Why: vitest reads its settings from this file, so adding one during GREEN can change the judge mid-game`
   `Next: remove <path>; if the suite needs it, add it in a RED (shipcraft red --redo) or after the cycle closes`
   `<place>` is `snapshot.describe()`, as `check_files` writes it; `<path>` and `<lock>` go through `red::one_line`. Bound locks are checked in their existing order, and for each lock the paths in bytewise order; the first found is named. It closes (B)'s other route: with `vite.config.ts` locked, a GREEN that adds a `vitest.config.ts` naming a new mock setup file is refused. Paths are checked in bytewise order and the first is named.

**Existing locks and configs.** No migration. A lock written before this release keeps its `files`, and the hook and CI judge it by them: a setup file it does not hold stays unjudged until the next `red` or `red --redo`; point 4 applies to it too (an added runner config is refused under an old lock). A lock written by this release is format 5 or 6 as today (no new format, no new key), so a 2.74.0 hook or CI judges it by its `files`, without point 4. `init` writes the same config and globs: the files are found at `red`, from the tree, so existing projects get them with no config change, and a setup file renamed between cycles is followed.

**The version: 2.75.0.** A fix, but not a patch: it adds two refusal codes (`setup-unreadable`, `runner-config-added`) and a line, and a project whose vitest config is built dynamically is refused at `red` where 2.74.0 locked. Nothing breaks a config, a lock or a command, and older binaries read the new locks, so it is minor. `init` writes `shipcraft_min = "2.75"`; existing configs keep theirs.

**Known limits.** Jest (`setupFiles`, `setupFilesAfterEach`, `globalSetup` in `jest.config.*` or `package.json`'s `jest` key) is a later item. So are pytest's own config (`pytest.ini`, `[tool.pytest.ini_options]` in `pyproject.toml`, `setup.cfg`, `tox.ini`), not locked today, and a vitest config passed with `--config` or `--root`, or named otherwise (`vitest.config.json`): its setup files are resolved from the config's folder, not from the invocation root, and a config outside a suite's root is not read. A workspace or `projects` entry that points at a `vite.config.*` in a subfolder, or at a config outside the suite's root, is not followed unless an import reaches it. A setup file that imports a helper outside the globs leaves that helper unlocked (imports are followed from config modules, not from setup files). Vite's own resolution (aliases, `index.*` in a folder) is not followed, and a new candidate created during GREEN with a higher-priority extension (`./boot` resolving to a new `boot.ts` beside a locked `boot.js`) is not judged. A key name built from parts (`['setup' + 'Files']`) and a shorthand `{ root }` are not seen. A regular expression or a string that only mentions `setupFiles` or `globalSetup` (outside a property) refuses `setup-unreadable`: rename it or move it out of the config. A nested project config (a `vitest.config.*` a workspace lists) is read with its own folder as the project root, which is vitest's default for a config file entry. Modules a config imports are part of the judge: a config that imports product code (`./src/env.ts` importing the feature) locks it, and the GREEN gets `test-changed` on it; the remedy is to keep the config's imports out of the product's import graph. A `vite.config.*` locked by point 1 is the app's build config too: a GREEN that must change it during a lock gets `test-changed`, and moving the test settings into `vitest.config.*` (without importing `vite.config`) unlocks it.

**Metric:** on the release, the reproduction's (A) and (B) GREEN commits are refused (`test-changed`), and POC-051 run 6's layout (`vitest.config.ts` naming `src/test-setup.ts`) locks the setup file. On Draken (`src/editorial-mcp/vitest.config.ts`, `globalSetup: ['./tests/helpers/firestore-emulator.ts']`) the next lock of that suite holds that file. False refusals: GREEN commits refused on a path points 1 or 4 added where the change was not a test shortcut, counted from each refused commit, over the next 10 locks on Hands-off and Draken (Hands-off's `app/packages/web/vite.config.ts`, with no `vitest.config.*` beside it, is now locked in its web suite), and `setup-unreadable` refusals over the same window. Revert: above 1 false refusal in those 10 locks, a release narrows point 1's `vite.config` rule to configs that hold a setup key; the setup-file rule stays.

**Touches.** Read first:
- **The version:** section 11 and `nucleo/Cargo.toml` go to 2.75.0 (`v1-62.version`, `v1-64.version`, `v1-67.version`, `v1-69.version`, `v1-87.version`, `v1-103.version`, `v1-108.version` and the others that read section 11, the manifest or `shipcraft_min`).
- **Assertions that change** (the RED adjusts exactly these):

The assertion becomes that the three are in `files`; nothing else in the case changes.

- **New cases** go in `acceptance/lib/item132.sh` (ids `v1-132.<name>`), with real git fixture repositories, never the network. Most cases use a fake `vitest` in `node_modules/.bin/` that writes JUnit and decides each case from files the fixture controls, so a setup file's bytes can make it pass, as (A) does. Two cases run the real vitest from the battery's npm cache, as items 51 and 71 do: reproduction (A), and (B)'s added-`vitest.config.ts` route refused by point 4. Estimate, 38: point 1, 19 (reproduction (A), real vitest; (B), `vite.config.ts` edited, refused `test-changed`; a single string value; `globalSetup`; a quoted key; a key in a comment locks nothing; an extensionless literal locks every extension present; a package literal and a `../` above the root lock nothing; a nested `vitest.config.ts` resolves from its folder; `vitest.workspace.ts` locked and read; `vitest.config.ts` importing `./vite.config` locks it, and one importing nothing leaves it unlocked; a `vite.config.ts` in a subfolder not locked; a two-level relative import followed and its setup key read; `node_modules` configs ignored; a symlinked setup file refuses `setup-unreadable`; a deleted setup file is `locked-file-deleted`; a suite whose command does not contain `vitest` gets nothing new, control; `red --redo` picks up a setup file added to the config); point 2, 8 (a variable value; `setupFiles.push`; a concatenation after a literal; a `require` of a built path; an absolute literal; a relative literal naming an ignored file; a root key other than `import.meta.dirname`, and `root:import.meta.dirname` accepted; the first unreadable config in path order named, and no lock written); point 3, 4 (the line's list, order and `and <n> more`; its place after the controls line, before `RED locked at`; on `red --redo`, and none on `RED unchanged`; none without new paths, byte for byte, control); point 4, 6 (real vitest: GREEN adds `vitest.config.ts`, refused `runner-config-added`; the same commit judged by `shipcraft ci` after `--no-verify`; the RED commit itself adds a `vite.config.ts` in a subfolder, accepted; a runner config already in a parent, unchanged, accepted; a lock written by 2.74.0's rules (without the setup file) still lets a setup edit through, no migration, control; an added `vite.config.ts` in a non-vitest suite, accepted, control); the RED may trade cases between points within 40. Round 2 of the spec review added cases the RED fits in the same 40, replacing the CRLF byte-keeping case: a CRLF file refused; a helper stub `:` refused; a helper line with another path refused; an existing call after `exit 0` refused; a helper line at the end of the file without `\n`; `npx shipcraft close` and `bunx shipcraft close` not recognised (the command is inserted); an `echo` launch line refused.

**Current behavior** on these paths (keep unless marked change):
- `red.rs`'s `locked_files` hashes `config.test_files(repo)` (paths of `repo.files()` that match a suite's globs and are files by `Path::is_file`) plus `.shipcraft.toml`, with `fs::read`, exiting 2 on a read error; `red` and `red --redo` call it twice (before and after item 53's second run). Change: point 1 adds paths, point 2's refusal.
- `red` prints item 53's dropped line, item 81's line, item 98's controls line, then `RED locked at <path>: <n> file(s), ...`, the commit line, item 59's and item 100's warnings and the `next:` line; `red --redo` the same with `RED redone at` and its three case lines, or `RED unchanged` and nothing else. Change: point 3's line, before `RED locked at`/`RED redone at`.
- `contract::check_files` refuses a locked path deleted (`locked-file-deleted`), `.shipcraft.toml` changed (`config-changed`) and any other locked path changed (`test-changed`); `check_lefthook` refuses hook integration files created, changed or removed against the parents for a bound lock that is not `Opened`; `ci` makes the same calls per commit, and item 46's redo admits any path of either lock. Keep: the new paths are judged as test files; change: point 4 after `check_lefthook`.
- `refuse_a_suite_without_tests` counts only paths that match a suite's globs. Keep. `suite::Selection` takes the lock's files through `of_suite`, which keeps those matching the suite's globs, and names to vitest those that hold a case. Keep: the new paths match no glob, so they are never named to the runner or written to `SHIPCRAFT_FILES`.
- `init`, the globs it writes, `docs.rs`'s protected paths and the measurement's test-line count read the globs. Keep.
- Standing effects the cases can meet, all kept:
  - Rows: `medicao::record` creates the shared git directory's `shipcraft/` folder and appends to `runs.jsonl` best effort (a failed append changes no exit code); the hook writes a row on every commit, even with no lock; `close` writes one row per lock it closes and none when it closes nothing.
  - Locks: `red` writes `.shipcraft/locks/<id>.json` and exits 2 when that write fails; `red --redo` writes a sibling `redo-<pid>.tmp`, renames it over the lock, and on a failed replacement removes it, refuses `redo-write-failed` and keeps the old lock. `init` writes `.shipcraft/locks/.gitkeep`.
  - Closing: `check --commit-msg` removes the previous per-worktree close mark, can refuse `mark-failed`, and writes a new mark for closing locks; plain `check` writes and stages the closed lock; the post-commit hook (`close`) makes `chore(shipcraft): close lock <id>`, or exits 0 with a manual-retry message, the mark kept and the lock still open, and reports an index or tree sync failure after HEAD moved.
  - Running: `red` runs `[build]`, checks and suites in the working tree with the caller's `TMPDIR`, and their outputs stay, also after a refusal. The hook runs the suite in the tree when nothing is outside the index and the commit has no index of its own, else on an export of the index (`Repo::export`: a temporary `shipcraft-arvore-*` folder under `TMPDIR` registered as a detached worktree, links to ignored dependencies, objects alternates for an unborn repository, removed best effort afterwards); `ci` runs in the tree when it is clean, exports HEAD for a dirty tree without `[build]`, and refuses `build-dirty-tree` with one. JUnit reports go to temporary files under `TMPDIR`, removed after reading; `SHIPCRAFT_FILES` and `SHIPCRAFT_CASES` lists (NUL-terminated) go under the git path `shipcraft/tmp`, removed after the run, a list folder `red` created empty is removed, and a failed list write leaves the variables out. Suite, check and build output goes to stderr.

### What the hook judges

- **Only the commit's content:** the index plus the tree's ignored files (dependencies, `.env`, generated files). An untracked, non-ignored file is left out, as in a clean clone. Changes outside the index never block and never change the result.
- **As a git checkout:** the copy it runs on has `HEAD`, history and `git ls-files`, like a clean clone.
- **Only the RED cases:** every locked file is protected by hash, but the hook executes only the test files that hold `failing_at_red` cases. Each suite's project-wide `check` runs in CI, not in the hook. An error outside the RED files does not block; CI reports it. When cases cannot be mapped to files, the hook runs all locked files but blocks only on RED cases.
- **The config:** a commit that changes `.shipcraft.toml` is validated, even with no lock open.
- **Without the hook's git variables (item 57).** Git runs a hook with `GIT_DIR` and `GIT_INDEX_FILE` set, and in some cases `GIT_WORK_TREE`. In the hook, every suite command `shipcraft` runs (the test command, on a copy or in the working tree) runs with exactly those three variables removed from its environment, whatever their values. They are removed from the shell `shipcraft` starts, so wrappers and their children inherit the cleaned environment. Every other variable is passed on as before, including signing and `GIT_CONFIG_*` settings and section 6's lists. `shipcraft`'s own git reads (the effective index, warnings, the close mark) keep the hook's context, as before.
  - **The index.** When `GIT_INDEX_FILE` names an index other than the repository's own (for example `git commit --only` or `git commit <paths>`), the hook runs the suite on a copy of that index, as it does when something is outside the index. A test that reads the index then sees the commit's content, never the ordinary index.
  - **What a test sees.** A test that runs git in the project finds the repository from its folder, as in a terminal. A test that runs `git init` in a folder of its own, and then works there, works on that repository, not on the project's. Git's upward discovery still applies: a temporary folder inside the project, before its own `git init`, belongs to the project.
  - **`red` and `ci`** are unchanged: they run from the user's shell, and the environment is the user's. A project whose git directory is only reachable through `GIT_DIR` keeps working there as before.

  Wrong-verdict note: since item 14, the hook removed them only when it ran on a copy (something outside the index). When nothing was, the suite ran in the working tree with the hook's variables. A test that ran `git init` in a temporary folder reinitialized the project's repository instead: in a linked worktree it set `core.bare = true` in the shared config, and every later git command in the worktrees failed (`fatal: this operation must be run in a work tree`). Found on 2026-10-28 by Shipcraft's own battery, at the first RED commit of the dogfooding trial (item 56). The battery should have caught it, but no case ran a suite that uses git through the hook's direct path.

Edge cases are fixed by the acceptance battery (`acceptance/`).

### What CI re-checks

`shipcraft ci --base <ref>` checks every commit of `<base>..HEAD` against the same rules (hashes, lock state, config, integration files). On HEAD only, it runs `[build]`, each suite's `check`, then the full suite. It does not install dependencies. It also refuses a green suite with a lock still open (`lock-not-closed`: the hook was skipped) and a failing suite with no open cycle (`suite-red`: this is where an old test broken by GREEN shows up).

## 3. Commands

Each command is `shipcraft <command>`. Exit codes, unless the row says otherwise: `0` success, `1` refusal (section 7), `2` failure (`shipcraft` could not do its job, or invalid arguments; the message starts with `shipcraft: error:`).

| Command | What it does |
|---|---|
| `--version` | Prints `shipcraft <X.Y.Z> (<12-char commit>)`, in any folder. |
| (no arguments) | Prints usage, exit `0`. |
| `init [--lang python\|node\|go] [--github]`, `init --lang other --command <cmd> --glob <glob>... [--github]` | Sets up the project (below; `other`, item 67). |
| `red [--redo] [--base <ref>] [--all]` | Runs the RED's changed test files, or every suite when it cannot narrow safely (item 55), requires assertion failures, reruns the failing cases once and does not lock those that pass (item 53), does not lock a failing case of a test file the RED did not change while one of its own fails (item 81), writes an open lock; `--redo` rewrites the branch's open lock (item 46). Refuses `lock-open`, `install-not-committed` (item 80), `red-already-passes`, `error-before-assertions`, `group-failed`, `red-not-reproduced`, `no-report`, `suite-without-tests`, `build-failed`. |
| `check [--commit-msg <file>]` | The hook: judges the commit against the open locks and closes them when the RED cases pass. With `--commit-msg` it judges the final index and writes a close mark instead of the lock (`mark-failed` if it cannot). |
| `close` | The `post-commit` half of the `commit-msg` route: if the mark matches the commit just made, adds one commit that only flips the lock to `closed`. On failure it changes nothing and says to rerun it. Always exits `0`. |
| `ci --base <ref> [--pr]` | CI (section 2). `--pr` allows the docs-only path (section 5). `ci` publishes no status (item 44). |
| `export --base <ref> <branch>` | For a project that does not use Shipcraft: writes `<branch>` with the commits of `<ref>..HEAD` without Shipcraft's files and without the commits that change only them, and names what it left out (item 135). Changes nothing else. Refuses `export-uses-shipcraft`, `export-lock-open`, `export-merge`, `export-branch-exists`, `export-empty`. |
| `pr [--title <t>] [--body <file>] [--merge]` | Pushes (`--force-with-lease`), opens or updates the PR, runs the layers, posts statuses and summary (section 5). Refuses `pr-not-on-branch`. Exit `0` is guaranteed only when every layer passed or is off. With `--merge`, once every layer passed it then attempts the merge exactly as `merge <pr>`, whose own gate decides the result (item 110). |
| `review` | Reviews local HEAD and prints the verdict; publishes nothing. Exit `0` on approval; refuses `review-changes-requested`, `review-error`, `review-off`. |
| `review --pr <n>` | Reviews any PR in an isolated copy and publishes `shipcraft/review` and a comment. Refuses `pr-from-fork`. |
| `merge <pr> [--no-review "<reason>"]` | The merge gate (section 5). Refuses `pr-not-open`, `merge-without-ci`, `merge-without-review`, `no-review-without-reason`. |
| `status` | Prints the layers, review agent (none when review is off, item 73), hook state, whether `AGENTS.md`'s block is this version's (item 89), open locks, and one line with the real branch protection level. |
| `report [--json] [--since <tag>] [--github]` | Prints the flow's numbers per lock and per day from the run log (section 8, item 38), with DORA from git (item 76); `--github` fills the waiting and merge times from GitHub (item 79). Reads only; refuses only bad arguments and a `--since` that is not a release. |
| `report --findings [--json] [--since <pr>]` | Prints the Blocking findings of the review comments on merged PRs, grouped by class, as the ledger `docs/review-findings.md` (item 121). Reads GitHub through `gh` only; refuses only bad arguments. |

`init` writes no run-log row; `red` and `close` write one each (item 38).

### `init`

`init` decides everything before it asks or writes. A refusal leaves the tree as it was; a write that fails midway is rolled back.

- **Language:** `pyproject.toml` or `requirements*.txt` is Python (pytest), `package.json` with vitest or jest is Node, `go.mod` is Go (gotestsum); `--lang` overrides. Workspaces (`pnpm-workspace.yaml`, `package.json` `workspaces`, `go.work`) get one `[[suite]]` per package with tests.
- **Config:** writes `.shipcraft.toml` with the test command, globs, `check` (`tsc --noEmit`, except for a tsconfig that checks no file, item 56; or `go vet`), `[execution]`, `[roles]`, `shipcraft_min` and, when it applies, `[build]`. Every `npx` it writes puts `--` before the tool's arguments.
- **Review:** `init` asks nothing. With an agent installed it writes `review = "local"` and the first one found in `[roles] review`, and says that the diff goes to that agent's provider and how to turn review off. With none installed, review is `off` and it says how to enable it.
- **AGENTS.md:** `init` creates `AGENTS.md` with a short block (marked `<!-- shipcraft -->`) telling agents the RED/GREEN workflow, or appends the block once to an existing file. A symlink or a failed write is left alone with a notice; it never fails the init. The block is the text `current_block()` builds in `nucleo/src/init/agents.rs`, in the form `agents_text(local)` picks.
- **Hook:** there is one route: `shipcraft check --commit-msg` in `commit-msg` and `shipcraft close` in `post-commit`, so the hook sees the index after formatters ran. `init` integrates only when it can prove which configuration runs the hook:
  - plain git: shipcraft's own `.git/hooks/commit-msg` and `.git/hooks/post-commit`;
  - lefthook: the two commands in the lefthook file's `commit-msg` and `post-commit` blocks;
  - husky 9 and the pre-commit framework: the two commands in their files.

  Anything `init` cannot prove (another tool's hook, `core.hooksPath` elsewhere, two hook managers, flow-style YAML, symlinks) refuses with `foreign-hook` and says where to add `shipcraft check` by hand. Edge cases are fixed by the acceptance battery (`acceptance/`).
- **GitHub:** `init` never writes a workflow. With `--github` it marks `shipcraft/ci` and, when review is on, `shipcraft/review` as required checks where GitHub offers branch protection.

### `init` for a project of any language (item 67)

`init` knows Python, Node and Go. In any other project it refuses `lang-unrecognized`, and the only way out was to write `.shipcraft.toml` by hand. That cuts the hook, the `AGENTS.md` block, the review and the roles off from every project in another language. Shipcraft's own repository is one of them: a Rust core with a shell battery, where `init` says "could not recognize the project language" (dogfood step 1, 2026-10-29). Shipcraft never needed to know the language: with `filter = "command"` any command that writes a JUnit report is a suite.

1. **`--lang other`.** `init --lang other --command '<command>' --glob '<glob>' [--glob '<glob>' ...]` sets up a project whose tests Shipcraft runs as written.
   - **`--command`** is required. It is a shell command, run as every suite command runs today (`sh -c`, at the suite's root). `{junit}` is replaced by the shell-quoted report path, as today, so it must appear as an unquoted shell word or part of one (`--junitxml={junit}`, not `--junitxml="{junit}"`). It must contain `{junit}` exactly once, must hold, once `{junit}` is removed, at least one character that is not whitespace by Rust's `char::is_whitespace`, and must have no control character as Rust's `char::is_control` defines it. `init` does not parse or run the command.
   - **`--glob`** is required at least once, and may repeat. Each value is a test or support file glob, validated and normalized as `test_globs` entries are today (relative, no `..`, no absolute path; `./` and empty components removed), and matched by Shipcraft's own glob matcher (`*`, `**`, `?`). It does not know braces `{a,b}` or character classes: they are taken literally. A vitest-style `**/*.{test,spec}.ts` is therefore two `--glob` values. After normalizing, repeats are dropped and the first one keeps its place.
   - **What it writes, when there is no `.shipcraft.toml` yet:** the top-level suite keys `test_command` and `test_globs`, with `filter = "command"`, and no `lang`, `runner`, `check` or `[build]`. `lang` stays limited to `python`, `node` and `go`, and is optional, as it is today for a hand-written config. Everything else is today's `init`: `[execution]`, `[roles]` (`review` as a string), `shipcraft_min`, the hook, the `AGENTS.md` block and its `CLAUDE.md` import, the review choice and the closing guidance. `init` skips TypeScript build inference entirely for `other`, so it runs no `tsc` and writes no `[build]`. It writes no stub.
   - **When `.shipcraft.toml` already exists,** it is kept as today: its suites and every other key stay as they are, and the only change is item 20.2's raise of `shipcraft_min` to the running binary's version, when that applies. The flags are still validated (point 2), but they do not change the config, and `init` prints `shipcraft: .shipcraft.toml exists and was kept; --command and --glob only seed a new config`. The closing guidance describes the kept config, as today.
   - Only `init --lang other` with valid flags takes this path. A plain `init` in a project whose language it cannot detect still refuses `lang-unrecognized`, even when a hand-written `.shipcraft.toml` exists.
   - The first `red` runs the command, and refuses `no-report` if it writes no report, as today.
2. **Refusals and their order.** Every refusal leaves the tree as it was, as `init` refusals do today. They come in this order:
   1. Command-line syntax, exit `2`: a repeated `--command`, a repeated `--lang`, or a flag without a value. A value that starts with `--` counts as missing, so `--command --glob x` is exit `2`.
   2. Today's legacy-config refusal, in its place.
   3. `lang-unknown`: `--command` or `--glob` given without `--lang other`, whether `--lang` names another language or is omitted. Also `--lang other` without `--command` or without any `--glob`, and a `--command` that breaks point 1's rules.
   4. `config-invalid`: the first invalid `--glob`, in argument order.
   5. Everything after that is today's `init` (hook planning, `foreign-hook` and the rest), with today's messages.
   
   The refusals in steps 3 and 4 end with `Next:` showing the full form: `shipcraft init --lang other --command '<test command writing JUnit to {junit}>' --glob '<test files>'`. No other refusal changes its `Next:`.
3. **Detection is unchanged.** With no `--lang`, detection still knows only Python, Node and Go. No language found still refuses `lang-unrecognized`, and more than one still refuses `lang-multiple`. Both add the full form of point 2 to their `Next:`. For `lang-unrecognized`, `Why:` adds that Shipcraft can run any command that writes JUnit.
4. **Touches.** These earlier items' cases cover what this item shares or changes. They must keep passing unchanged:
   - language and init output: `init-detect`, `init-files`, `init-workflow`, `init-lang-flag` (`run.sh`), item 16b's `init --lang node` and its TypeScript build inference (`acceptance/lib/item16b.sh`, `pontos/16b.md`), and item 25's `init --lang python`;
   - workspaces: `v1-9.1-init-pnpm`, `v1-9.1-init-workspaces-do-package-json`, `v1-9.1-init-go-work`;
   - checks and npx: `v1-13.1-init-escreve-verifica`, `v1-13.1-init-verifica-com-traco`, `v1-12.1-npx-tsc-com-traco`, `v1-12.1-todo-npx-com-traco`, and item 56;
   - globs and `--github`: item 30's glob normalization and rejection cases (`v1-30.1-glob-*`, `v1-30.1-v0-glob-*`, `v1-30.3-*`), `v1-5.3-init-github-marca-obrigatorios` and `v1-5.3-init-github-plano-gratis`;
   - refusal order and rollback: `v1-15a.1-recusa-antes-de-perguntar-e-escrever`, `v1-15a.1-decide-antes-e-desfaz-escrita-no-meio`, `v1-15a.1-falha-preserva-config-simbolica`, `r-legacy-config-init`, `r-legacy-config-coexists`;
   - hooks: `v1-20.rota.commit-msg`, `v1-20.rota.post-commit`, `v1-58.plain`, `v1-58.kept`, `v1-58.rollback`;
   - guidance and comments: `v1-27.mensagem-local`, `v1-27.mensagem-desligado`, `v1-27.mensagem-github`, `v1-62.init`, `v1-62.init-preserve`, and `v1-2.3-tela-do-init` (init asks nothing);
   - item 61's `AGENTS.md` block and item 64's `[roles]` defaults.
   
   No existing case asserts the text of `lang-unrecognized` or `lang-multiple`, or uses `--command` or `--glob`. README fixed points 14 and 15 still describe a review question that `init` no longer asks (item 25 removed it); they are corrected to "init asks nothing". No assertion changes.
5. **Measure and revert.**
   - **Metric:** `init --lang other` on Shipcraft's own repository (with `.shipcraft.toml`, `.shipcraft/` and the hooks moved aside) writes a config whose suite matches ours: the same command and globs, and `filter = "command"`. It is also the onboarding path for adopters in other languages.
   - **Threshold:** that run succeeds, plus 0 false refusals over the next 5 items.
   - **Revert:** a release. A project already set up keeps its config.
6. **Known limits.**
   - Shipcraft does not filter a command it does not know, so the hook runs the whole command on every commit with an open lock, unless the command reads `SHIPCRAFT_FILES` or `SHIPCRAFT_CASES` itself (section 6).
   - `init` writes no stub for a language it does not know.
   - Rust's `cargo test` writes no JUnit by itself. A Rust project needs a reporter such as `cargo nextest` with a JUnit profile, named in `--command`.

### `init` writes no check that checks nothing (item 56)

Before item 56, `init` wrote `check = "tsc --noEmit"` for every Node suite with a `tsconfig.json`. A *solution* tsconfig, `"files": []` with `references` to the real projects, makes that command check no file: `tsc` reads no source and the check always passes. The Forge's root tsconfig is such a file (POC-019). The project type-checks with `pnpm tsc -b && pnpm typecheck:tests`.

- **What is read.** `init` reads the suite folder's `tsconfig.json` from the working tree, tracked or not, as suite detection does. A symlink, a file that cannot be read, or one the tsconfig parser that `[build]` uses (JSON with comments and trailing commas) cannot parse, is not judged: `init` writes `tsc --noEmit` as today.
- **What checks nothing.** The file is a JSON object, and:
  - its `files` key holds an empty array;
  - its `include` key is absent or holds an empty array;
  - its `extends` key is absent (any value of it, `null` included, means not judged);
  - its `references` key holds a non-empty array.
  Any other value of any of these keys means not judged.
- **What `init` writes then.** It depends on `[build]`, which is decided first, as today:
  - **`[build]` written** (its `tsc -b` succeeded and includes this suite's folder): no `check` key for that suite. `[build]` type-checks the referenced projects whenever it runs. `init` prints one line: `shipcraft: <path> checks no file; no check written for suite <name>: [build] type-checks its references`.
  - **`[build]` not written** (its command failed, as today's warning says): `check = "tsc --noEmit"` as today, so no gate is lost. `init` prints one line: `shipcraft: <path> checks no file; the check of suite <name> passes without checking: set check to the project's own type-check command`.
  - `<path>` is the tsconfig's path relative to the repository (`tsconfig.json` at the root). `<name>` is the generated suite name, or `default` for the top-level single suite. Control characters in either are escaped as in item 52's lists. The line comes after the `[build]` lines, one per affected suite, in suite order, on stdout.
- **Unchanged.** Every other suite and language. A tsconfig that lists files, has a non-empty `include` or has `extends`. An existing `.shipcraft.toml`, which `init` does not rewrite, prints no line. `init` still exits `0`. `init` writes no run-log row.
- **Battery.** README fixed points 40 and 57 (a check wherever a tsconfig exists) get item 56's exception. `v1-16b.1-build-reference`, whose solution tsconfig references a missing folder and whose build fails, keeps every assertion: `[build]` is not written, so its check stays. It gains the second line only. `v1-13.1-*` and `v1-12.1-*`, whose tsconfigs have a non-empty `include`, are unchanged.
- **Known limits.**
  - `init` does not follow `extends`. A tsconfig whose effective files and include, after inheritance, are empty still gets `tsc --noEmit`, which checks nothing.
  - The judgement is per suite folder. A root solution tsconfig in a workspace whose root holds no tests is not a suite and is not read; `[build]` still covers it when written.
  - A solution tsconfig without `references` (`"files": []` alone) is not judged. `tsc` reports it as an error, so today's check already fails there.

### No per-suite `tsc --noEmit` where `[build]` type-checks the suite's folder (item 72)

Wrong-verdict note: since item 16b (2.0.0), `init` writes `[build]` (`tsc -b` on every folder whose tracked `tsconfig.json` has `references` or `composite: true`) and still writes `check = "tsc --noEmit"` for each Node suite with a `tsconfig.json`. Item 56 removed the check only where the tsconfig checks no file. Found on the Forge, the second adopter (2.25.0, deluge-forge-platform PR #805, 2026-10-31): its packages' tsconfigs are composite projects with references, and `tsc --noEmit` there is refused with TS6310 (a referenced project may not disable emit), so every `red` and `ci` would refuse on the check. The Forge removed every check by hand. Item 56's spec review should have caught it: it judged the solution tsconfig only.

1. **The rule.** The only input is the folder list item 16b's inference returns when it writes `[build]` (the root as `.`): the folders its own reader selected (tracked `tsconfig.json` read from the index, workspace manifests and globs as item 16b reads them) and its proof run accepted. No tsconfig is read again for this rule, and a folder with the signal that inference did not name (outside the workspace globs, untracked, or in the working tree only) does not count. A Node suite whose package folder is one of those folders gets no `check` key, whatever its `tsconfig.json` holds. The package folder is the suite's root, or, for a suite item 71 converted to root `.`, the package folder `<dir>` its check enters.
2. **What `init` prints.** One line per such suite, in suite order, on stdout, buffered and printed where item 56's lines are printed today (after the core writes succeed, after the `[build]` lines, in the same position), never on a refused or rolled-back `init`:
   - when item 56's reader judges the suite's tsconfig as checking no file: item 56's line, unchanged (`shipcraft: <path> checks no file; no check written for suite <name>: [build] type-checks its references`);
   - for every other shape of the file (a project with sources, an `extends`, a file item 56 does not judge): `shipcraft: no check written for suite <name>: [build] type-checks <path>`.
   `<path>` and `<name>` are item 56's (the tsconfig's path from the repository root, `default` for the top-level suite, control characters escaped).
3. **When `[build]` is not written** (the proof run failed, a manifest or tsconfig did not read, a glob was not understood, as item 16b says), every suite keeps today's behavior: `tsc --noEmit` where a `tsconfig.json` exists, and item 56's second line where it checks no file.
4. **Where `[build]` runs.** As today: in `red`'s first run and in `ci`, before any suite. The hook and `red`'s confirmation run do not run `[build]` or checks; this item does not change that.
5. **Current behavior** on the paths this item touches (rule 14):
   - `[build]` inference: its readers (index, JSONC, manifests, globs), its proof run with real `tsc -b`, whose output (`dist/`, declarations, `.tsbuildinfo`, partial output on failure) stays in the working tree on success, on a failed proof and on a later failed `init`; its one-line JSONC diagnostic, its three-line warnings for manifests, globs and a failing compiler, after which `init` still succeeds without `[build]`; a git or process I/O error, which fails `init` as today: keep.
   - `check = "<bin>tsc --noEmit"` for a Node suite with a `tsconfig.json` whose root `[build]` does not name: keep.
   - Item 56's reader, its judgement and both its lines: keep.
   - A Node suite whose root `[build]` names and whose tsconfig item 56 does not judge as checking no file: gets `tsc --noEmit` today; change, as in point 1, with point 2's new line.
   - The rollback on a failed core write (only paths this run created, `shipcraft: undid what this run created`, the underlying error), no run-log row, `.git/shipcraft` untouched: keep.
   - Python, Go, `--lang other`, jest and vitest commands, the globs, an existing `.shipcraft.toml` (not rewritten, no line), `init`'s exit code, the closing guidance, the hooks, the block and the briefs: keep.
6. **Touches.**
   - **Assertions that change:** item 56's cases whose fixture gets a written `[build]` that names the suite's folder while item 56 does not judge the tsconfig as checking no file: today they require `check` ending in `tsc --noEmit`; they now require no check and point 2's second line. The review names at least `v1-56.extends` and `v1-56.files`; the RED runs every `v1-56.*` mode against today's binary, lists each mode where `[build]` names the suite folder, and changes exactly those (`acceptance/tools/item56.py`), keeping every assertion of item 56's solution modes. README fixed points 40 and 57 (a check wherever a tsconfig exists) gain this exception next to item 56's. Item 16b's cases (`v1-16b.1-*`) where a suite's own folder is a `[build]` folder with a check expected change the same way; the RED lists them.

   - **Must keep passing unchanged:** every other `v1-56.*` and `v1-16b.1-*` case; item 12's and 13's TypeScript init cases (`v1-12.1-*`, `v1-13.1-init-escreve-verifica`, `v1-13.1-init-verifica-com-traco`, `v1-13.1-verifica-fora-do-hook`) unless the RED shows their fixture's folder is a `[build]` folder; item 16a's hook cases (`v1-16a.1-hook-saida-ignorada`, `v1-16a.1-hook-sem-saida`) and README fixed point 59; item 9's workspace cases; `init-files`; `v1-15a.1-decide-antes-e-desfaz-escrita-no-meio`, `v1-15a.1-falha-preserva-config-simbolica`; `v1-58.plain`, `v1-58.kept`, `v1-58.rollback`; `v1-64.init-codex`, `v1-64.init-claude`, `v1-64.init-gemini`, `v1-64.init-none`, `v1-64.init-preserve`; item 25's closing lines; item 67's and 68's init cases.
7. **Measure and revert.**
   - **Metric:** on the Forge's layout, `init` writes no per-suite check and `[build]` covers every package; 0 adopter suites refused with TS6310 over the next 5 adopter inits.
   - **Revert:** a release restores the check; projects keep their config.
8. **Known limits.**
   - Without a written `[build]`, the kept `tsc --noEmit` on a solution tsconfig checks nothing (item 56's second line says so); the user sets the project's own type-check command. This item does not change that.
   - The hook does not run `[build]`, so a type-only error in a covered folder is caught by `red`'s first run or `ci`, not at commit, as for any `[build]` today.
   - A project whose `[build]` is later edited by hand to cover fewer folders keeps no check for the folders it dropped; `init` does not rewrite an existing config.

### `init` for a project-local install (item 58)

Before item 58, `init` wired the hooks to the binary that ran it: plain git hooks named its absolute path, and lefthook, husky and the pre-commit framework a bare `shipcraft` found on `PATH`. Every project on a machine then used the machine's one binary. Upgrading it upgraded every project at once and without a commit: draken-platform ran three versions in one day (2026-10-28) while its owner believed it was on 2.11.0. A Node project can instead pin Shipcraft as a dev dependency (`pnpm add -D shipcraft`), so the version is in its lockfile.

- **When the install is the project's.**
  - `init` canonicalizes two paths with the filesystem (symlinks resolved): the binary running it, and the repository's working-tree root as `git rev-parse --show-toplevel` gives it.
  - The install is the project's when the binary's path, compared component by component, is under `<root>/node_modules/`, and `<root>/node_modules/.bin/shipcraft`, with symlinks followed, is a regular file the user can execute.
  - Otherwise the hooks are wired as today, and nothing new is printed: an install elsewhere (`install.sh`, `cargo install`), a path that cannot be canonicalized, or a `.bin/shipcraft` that is missing, not a regular file or not executable.
  - An install under a package's `node_modules` (`packages/x/node_modules`) also keeps today's wiring, with one line: `shipcraft: installed under <dir>/node_modules; hooks call this binary by its path (only the root node_modules is wired as the project's)`.
- **What `init` writes then.** These are new integrations only. Integrations that already exist are kept as today.
  - **Plain git:**
    - The `commit-msg` hook runs `"$(git rev-parse --show-toplevel)/node_modules/.bin/shipcraft" check --commit-msg "$1"`. When that file is not executable, it prints `shipcraft: not installed in this checkout (node_modules/.bin/shipcraft): install the project's dependencies` and exits `1`: the commit is refused.
    - The `post-commit` hook runs `"…/node_modules/.bin/shipcraft" close`. When that file is not executable, it prints `shipcraft: not installed in this checkout (node_modules/.bin/shipcraft): the lock stays open; install the project's dependencies and run node_modules/.bin/shipcraft close` and exits `0`, as `close` does.
    - Both keep the `# shipcraft hook` marker.
  - **lefthook, husky 9, pre-commit framework:** the same commands as today, with `shipcraft` replaced by `./node_modules/.bin/shipcraft`, each tool forwarding the message file as today.
  - Before its closing guidance lines (items 25 and 27), `init` prints `shipcraft: hooks call this project's install (node_modules/.bin/shipcraft); upgrade it with your package manager`. It prints this only when it wrote these hooks.
- **Recognition.** `status` and a repeated `init` recognize the generated commands in both forms, `shipcraft …` and `./node_modules/.bin/shipcraft …`, and the plain hooks in both forms. They do so whether or not the dependencies are installed when they look. This is the one change outside `init`.
- **Unchanged.**
  - `shipcraft_min` is still written as the running binary's `major.minor`.
  - Every other command, the run log, and the hooks and configs of projects already integrated.
  - To move an integrated project to its own install, remove the old hooks and run `init` again.
- **Known limits.**
  - **Clones need their own `init`.** A clone does not receive `.git/hooks`. Each clone runs `init` once (or the hook manager's install) after installing its dependencies. Until then nothing runs at commit, and CI is the backstop, as for any clone today.
  - **Node at commit time.** The package's `.bin/shipcraft` is a Node launcher, so the project-local install needs Node when committing.
  - **Linked worktrees share one hook folder.** That works when every worktree has its own `node_modules` at its root, which a package manager install gives.
  - **The launcher and the binary can differ.** `.bin/shipcraft` is whatever the package manager linked there. If a nested dependency carries a different version, the hooks call the root one, not necessarily the one that ran `init`.
  - **Hook managers that run from another folder.** A manager that does not run hooks from the repository root does not find the relative path; lefthook's `root:` option is one case.

### Onboarding the coding agents (item 61)

POC-021 (2026-10-29, Shipcraft 2.14.0 from npm) asked Claude Code and Codex, in a fresh session each, to install Shipcraft in two sample projects and then add a small feature. No run closed a cycle. 3 of 4 runs stopped at `pnpm add -D shipcraft` and never ran `init`; each checked the install with `shipcraft --version` or `--help`. In the one run where `init` wrote the `AGENTS.md` block, Claude ran `red` unprompted, citing the block, but did RED and GREEN in one session and committed nothing. Item 61 changes what those touch points say.

**Reading the two files.** `AGENTS.md` and `CLAUDE.md` at the repository root are read as UTF-8; a file that is not valid UTF-8 is a read failure (kept, with today's notice). A *line* ends at `\n`; a line *is a marker* when, without a trailing `\r`, it equals `<!-- shipcraft -->` (the start line) or `<!-- /shipcraft -->` (the end line) exactly, wherever it is (inside a fenced code block too). A *block* is the bytes from the first byte of a start line through the end of the next end line, its `\n` included when present.

- **The block asks for separate sessions and commits.**
  - The new block is the text `current_block()` builds in `nucleo/src/init/agents.rs` (in the form `agents_text(local)` picks), from a start line to an end line, LF line endings. Besides today's rules it says, in this order:
    - the RED and the GREEN are done by different agent sessions: in Claude Code a subagent per phase, in Codex a new `codex exec` session per phase (never `resume`), both in the same checkout;
    - the RED session commits the tests, the stubs they need and the lock in one commit, and stops;
    - the GREEN session starts from that commit, may change anything the open lock does not protect (product code, dependencies and their lockfiles, config), never a locked file, and commits; a test it finds wrong is reported back, and a RED session fixes it with `red --redo` (item 46);
    - a session told it is the RED or the GREEN does that phase only and starts no other session; the session the user talks to starts them;
    - today's rule on locks reads: never edit or delete `.shipcraft/locks/*` by hand (`shipcraft red`, `red --redo` and the hook write them).
  - **The command it names.** When `init` runs from a project-local install (item 58's rule), every `shipcraft` command in the block is written `npx -- shipcraft` (fixed point 39's `--`), and the block says to run Shipcraft commands from the repository root, where `npx` resolves the root install item 58 wires the hooks to; otherwise `shipcraft`. These are the block's two *current variants*.
  - **Known blocks.** The current variants, plus the 2.14.0 block: the exact bytes of `AGENTS_BLOCK` at tag `v2.14.0`, which has a start line and no end line. Each is kept in the binary.
  - **What `init` does with `AGENTS.md`.**
    - No start line: the new block is appended, with today's separator rule (or the file is created).
    - Exactly one start line, followed by exactly one end line and no other marker, and the block between them byte-equal to a known block: the block is replaced by the new one; every byte before and after is kept. Equal to the new block: kept, `already has the shipcraft block`, as today.
    - Exactly one start line, no end line, and the bytes from the start line equal to the 2.14.0 block followed by anything: those bytes are replaced by the new block; what follows is kept.
    - Anything else (more markers, a block that is not a known one, an edited 2.14.0 block): kept, with today's `already has the shipcraft block, kept` plus `; it differs from this version's: edit it by hand to match docs/spec.md`.
    - A symlink or any entry that is not a regular file, and read or write failures: as today (kept, notice, never failing the init).
    - **Writing.** A new file is created as today. An existing file is rewritten through a temporary file in the same folder, given the old file's permission bits, then renamed over it; any failure removes the temporary file and leaves the old bytes. A file whose permission bits forbid the owner to write is kept, with the write-failure notice.
- **`CLAUDE.md` imports the block.** Claude Code reads `CLAUDE.md`, and reads another file through an `@path` import line.
  - `init` handles `CLAUDE.md` at the repository root when an entry exists there (`lstat` succeeds: a symlink, dangling or not, counts and is then kept as `AGENTS.md`'s are; `lstat` failing with anything but not-found also counts, and the entry is kept with the read-failure notice), or when `claude` is found on `PATH` by the same lookup `init` uses to find review agents (checked on its own, whatever `[roles] review` is).
  - Its block is three lines: the start line, `Shipcraft workflow (required): @AGENTS.md`, the end line. The rules are `AGENTS.md`'s with this block as the only known block: created, appended, kept equal, kept different with the notice.
  - `CLAUDE.md` is handled only when `AGENTS.md` is a regular file that holds the current block after `init` (written now or already equal); a symlinked `AGENTS.md` never qualifies. Otherwise `init` leaves `CLAUDE.md` untouched and, only when `CLAUDE.md` is handled by the rule above, prints `shipcraft: CLAUDE.md not written: AGENTS.md does not hold this version's shipcraft block`.
  - `init`'s closing line lists each of the two files it created, appended to or replaced, as it lists `AGENTS.md` today.
- **`--version` and usage say when the config is missing.** After their usual output, `shipcraft --version` and the usage (no arguments, `help`, `-h`, `--help`) print one line to stderr when the current folder is inside a git work tree (`git rev-parse --show-toplevel` succeeds) and `lstat` of `.shipcraft.toml` at its root fails with not-found: `shipcraft: not set up in this repository: run shipcraft init (from a package install: pnpm exec shipcraft init, or npx shipcraft init)`. In every other case (outside a work tree, git fails, the entry exists in any form, `lstat` fails otherwise) nothing is added. They read no config and write no run-log row; their stdout and exit codes do not change.
- **Docs.** The README's quick start and the npm package README say that installing is two steps: add the package, then run `init`.

**Unchanged.** The block's existing rules, the hook, `red`, every refusal, the config `init` writes, and `init`'s refusals: a refused `init` writes neither file.

**Battery.**
- `v1-2.5-agents-novo`, `v1-2.5-agents-acrescenta`, `v1-2.5-agents-uma-vez` and `v1-2.5-agents-link` must hold.
- `python::init-files`, `node::init-files` and `go::init-files` (`acceptance/run.sh`) reject any file `init` writes besides `.shipcraft.toml`, the locks folder and `AGENTS.md`, and keep the caller's `PATH`: on a machine with `claude` on `PATH`, `init` now writes `CLAUDE.md`. The RED makes them independent of the machine (a `PATH` without `claude`, or `CLAUDE.md` allowed when it is on `PATH`), without weakening the rest.
- `v1-21.versao-fora` and `v1-21.versao-min-numerica` run `--version` outside a repository and must hold.

**Versions.** The item raises the binary to 2.17.0. No lock or config field changes.

**Known limits.**
- Whether an agent follows the block is measured by POC-021's after runs, not by the battery.
- Codex and other agents read `AGENTS.md` directly; an agent that reads neither file is not covered.
- A `CLAUDE.md` in a subfolder, or a user-level `~/.claude/CLAUDE.md`, is not read or written.
- A block edited by hand, or written with CRLF line endings, is never upgraded.
- Markers inside a fenced code block count as a block. A `CLAUDE.md` whose only import block sits inside a fence is kept, and Claude Code, which skips imports in code blocks, does not read `AGENTS.md` through it.
- The hint covers a repository with no config. A fresh clone of a project that has one, but whose hooks are not installed yet, gets no hint; CI is the backstop, as today.
- `npx -- shipcraft` needs Node at the agent's shell, as item 58's hooks do, and resolves the root install only from the repository root; an agent that runs it inside a workspace package with its own older install gets that one.

### The block carries the flow's rules, and `init` installs the phase briefs (item 68)

Three rules that make our own RED/GREEN cycle work lived only in our private process notes, so an adopter's agents never saw them:
- **Read the tests the change touches.** All 5 wrong RED tests of items 64 to 66 contradicted behavior an earlier test had already fixed.
- **A wrong locked test goes to a new RED session that does not see the GREEN's code.** A test fixed while looking at the implementation tends to fit the implementation.
- **Start each phase with the role the project chose** (item 64's `[roles]`).

Victor's instruction for the dogfood was "the rules in the product, not in memory". Item 68 puts these rules in the `AGENTS.md` block, and gives each phase a short brief that `init` installs in the project.
